Critical Exponents of Three-Dimensional Percolation JAH-USEQPR-000001 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The critical exponents of three-dimensional percolation describe how the model behaves near its phase transition. When bonds of the cubic lattice are open independently with probability p, an infinite open cluster appears above a critical probability p_c. Near p_c, the density of the infinite cluster grows like (p - p_c) to the power beta, the correlation length diverges like |p - p_c| to the power minus nu, and the mean cluster size diverges with exponent gamma. Numerical estimates exist, but no exact values of these exponents are known in three dimensions, and the problem is open. The central equation is: theta(p) ~ (p - p_c)^beta, xi ~ |p - p_c|^{-nu}: what are the exact values of beta, nu, gamma in 3D? Percolation exponents govern how fluids move through rock, how epidemics spread through networks, how materials fracture and how forests burn: every system with a connectivity threshold shares these numbers. Exact values would confirm the renormalisation group picture of critical phenomena in three dimensions and would give precise predictions for porous media, composite materials and network resilience. The exponents are also a test case for the mathematical theory of phase transitions, where two dimensions is understood and three dimensions remains mysterious. Their determination would be a landmark in probability and physics alike.

Statement

The critical exponents of three-dimensional percolation describe how the model behaves near its phase transition. When bonds of the cubic lattice are open independently with probability p, an infinite open cluster appears above a critical probability p_c. Near p_c, the density of the infinite cluster grows like (p - p_c) to the power beta, the correlation length diverges like |p - p_c| to the power minus nu, and the mean cluster size diverges with exponent gamma. Numerical estimates exist, but no exact values of these exponents are known in three dimensions, and the problem is open. The equation is: theta(p) ~ (p - p_c)^beta, xi ~ |p - p_c|^{-nu}: what are the exact values of beta, nu, gamma in 3D?

History

Broadbent and Hammersley introduced percolation in 1957 to model fluid flow through porous rock. Kesten proved in 1980 that the bond threshold on the square lattice is one half, and the two-dimensional exponents were later derived exactly through conformal field theory and Schramm-Loewner evolution, with the one-arm exponent 5/48 proved by Lawler, Schramm and Werner. In three dimensions no conformal invariance is available, and the exponents have been estimated only by large-scale simulation and series expansion since the 1970s, with values such as nu around 0.877 and beta around 0.417, but no exact derivation exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the critical exponents exist in three dimensions and satisfy scaling relations such as 2 beta + gamma = d nu, by the work of Aizenman, Barsky and Menshikov on the subcritical phase and Grimmett and Marstrand on the supercritical phase. High-precision numerical estimates give nu = 0.8762, beta = 0.4173 and gamma = 1.795, each with small error bars, and the exponents are believed to be universal across lattices in the same dimension. No exact values are known, no proof of the scaling relations at the critical point itself is complete in three dimensions, and the exponents remain among the most wanted numbers in statistical physics.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000001 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let theta(p) be the probability that the origin lies in an infinite open cluster, and let xi(p) be the correlation length. The exponents are defined by theta(p) ~ (p - p_c)^beta as p approaches p_c from above, and xi(p) ~ |p - p_c|^{-nu}. In metamath-style gloss: there exist constants beta, nu in (0, infinity) such that the logarithm of theta(p) divided by the logarithm of (p - p_c) tends to beta, and similarly for xi. The problem asks for the exact real numbers beta, nu and gamma, and for a proof that these limits exist with those values in three dimensions.

Science Proof

Percolation exponents govern how fluids move through rock, how epidemics spread through networks, how materials fracture and how forests burn: every system with a connectivity threshold shares these numbers. Exact values would confirm the renormalisation group picture of critical phenomena in three dimensions and would give precise predictions for porous media, composite materials and network resilience. The exponents are also a test case for the mathematical theory of phase transitions, where two dimensions is understood and three dimensions remains mysterious. Their determination would be a landmark in probability and physics alike.

The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Program

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

Broadbent and Hammersley introduced percolation in 1957 to model fluid flow through porous rock. Kesten proved in 1980 that the bond threshold on the square lattice is one half, and the two-dimensional exponents were later derived exactly through conformal field theory and Schramm-Loewner evolution, with the one-arm exponent 5/48 proved by Lawler, Schramm and Werner. In three dimensions no conformal invariance is available, and the exponents have been estimated only by large-scale simulation and series expansion since the 1970s, with values such as nu around 0.877 and beta around 0.417, but no exact derivation exists. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of probability, statistical physics and random structures, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The critical exponents of three-dimensional percolation describe how the model behaves near its phase transition. When bonds of the cubic lattice are open independently with probability p, an infinite open cluster appears above a critical probability p_c. Near p_c, the density of the infinite cluster grows like (p - p_c) to the power beta, the correlation length diverges like |p - p_c| to the power minus nu, and the mean cluster size diverges with exponent gamma. Numerical estimates exist, but no exact values of these exponents are known in three dimensions, and the problem is open. In symbols, the problem is: theta(p) ~ (p - p_c)^beta, xi ~ |p - p_c|^{-nu}: what are the exact values of beta, nu, gamma in 3D? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from probability, statistical physics and random structures. It is known that the critical exponents exist in three dimensions and satisfy scaling relations such as 2 beta + gamma = d nu, by the work of Aizenman, Barsky and Menshikov on the subcritical phase and Grimmett and Marstrand on the supercritical phase. High-precision numerical estimates give nu = 0.8762, beta = 0.4173 and gamma = 1.795, each with small error bars, and the exponents are believed to be universal across lattices in the same dimension. No exact values are known, no proof of the scaling relations at the critical point itself is complete in three dimensions, and the exponents remain among the most wanted numbers in statistical physics. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1001
N = 6
P = 0.5


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    grid = [[rng.random() < P for _ in range(N)] for _ in range(N)]
    open_cells = sum(sum(row) for row in grid)
    seen = set()
    stack = [(0, j) for j in range(N) if grid[0][j]]
    while stack:
        x, y = stack.pop()
        if (x, y) in seen:
            continue
        seen.add((x, y))
        for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            if 0 <= nx < N and 0 <= ny < N and grid[nx][ny] and (nx, ny) not in seen:
                stack.append((nx, ny))
    spanning = any((N - 1, j) in seen for j in range(N))
    payload = "perc n=%d p=%s open=%d cluster=%d spanning=%s" % (N, P, open_cells, len(seen), spanning)
    lines = ["Grid: %dx%d, p=%s" % (N, N, P),
             "Open cells: %d" % open_cells,
             "Top cluster size: %d" % len(seen),
             "Spanning cluster: %s" % spanning]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

import random

SEED = 1001
N = 6
P = 0.5


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    grid = [[rng.random() < P for _ in range(N)] for _ in range(N)]
    open_cells = sum(sum(row) for row in grid)
    seen = set()

    while stack:
        x, y = stack.pop()
        if (x, y) in seen:
            continue
        seen.add((x, y))
        for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            if 0 <= nx < N and 0 <= ny < N and grid[nx][ny] and (nx, ny) not in seen:
                stack.append((nx, ny))
    spanning = any((N - 1, j) in seen for j in range(N))
    payload = "perc n=%d p=%s open=%d cluster=%d spanning=%s" % (N, P, open_cells, len(seen), spanning)

             "Open cells: %d" % open_cells,
             "Top cluster size: %d" % len(seen),
             "Spanning cluster: %s" % spanning]
    return payload, lines

Exact Critical Probability of Bond Percolation on Z^3 JAH-USEQPR-000002 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The exact critical probability of bond percolation on the three-dimensional cubic lattice Z^3 is unknown. Bonds of the lattice are open independently with probability p, and p_c is the threshold above which an infinite open cluster exists with probability one and below which it does not. Simulations estimate p_c at approximately 0.248812, but no exact closed form is known, and no proof determines the value. The problem asks for the exact number, or a proof of what kind of number it is, and it is one of the oldest open quantitative questions in probability. The central equation is: p_c(Z^3, bond) = ? (the exact threshold for bond percolation on the cubic lattice) The critical probability is the fundamental constant of three-dimensional connectivity: it tells engineers and physicists exactly when a random porous material conducts, when a random network stays connected, and when a fluid can pass through rock. Its exact value would test the methods of rigorous probability against high-precision simulation, and would clarify whether critical probabilities are computable numbers with closed forms or intrinsically numerical constants. The problem also stands for a whole family of unknown thresholds in three dimensions, where two-dimensional duality fails and new mathematics is needed.

Statement

The exact critical probability of bond percolation on the three-dimensional cubic lattice Z^3 is unknown. Bonds of the lattice are open independently with probability p, and p_c is the threshold above which an infinite open cluster exists with probability one and below which it does not. Simulations estimate p_c at approximately 0.248812, but no exact closed form is known, and no proof determines the value. The problem asks for the exact number, or a proof of what kind of number it is, and it is one of the oldest open quantitative questions in probability. The equation is: p_c(Z^3, bond) = ? (the exact threshold for bond percolation on the cubic lattice)

History

Broadbent and Hammersley introduced percolation in 1957 and asked for critical probabilities. Kesten proved in 1980 that the bond threshold on the square lattice Z^2 is exactly one half, using planar duality, a result that took more than twenty years. For the cubic lattice there is no duality to exploit, and the threshold has been estimated by Monte Carlo simulation, series expansion and rigorous bounds since the 1960s. The best rigorous bounds confine p_c to a narrow interval around 0.2488, but the interval has not collapsed to a point, and no exact value has been found.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that p_c for bond percolation on Z^3 lies in a rigorous interval around 0.2488, with the best simulations giving 0.2488126 with tiny statistical error. For site percolation on Z^3 the threshold is approximately 0.3116, also unknown exactly. Exact values are known only in special cases: one half for bond percolation on Z^2, and 1/(z-1) for a regular tree of degree z. The threshold is known to exist and to be strictly between zero and one by general arguments, and the uniqueness of the infinite cluster above p_c is proved, but the exact numerical value resists every method.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000002 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let each bond of Z^3 be open independently with probability p, and let theta(p) be the probability that the origin belongs to an infinite open cluster. The critical probability is p_c = inf{p: theta(p) > 0}. In metamath-style gloss: p_c is the least real number such that for all p > p_c, theta(p) > 0, and for all p < p_c, theta(p) = 0. The problem asks for the exact real number p_c, or at least for a proof that it equals some explicit expression in known constants. No such expression is known, and the value is believed to be a non-algebraic number with no simple form.

Science Proof

The critical probability is the fundamental constant of three-dimensional connectivity: it tells engineers and physicists exactly when a random porous material conducts, when a random network stays connected, and when a fluid can pass through rock. Its exact value would test the methods of rigorous probability against high-precision simulation, and would clarify whether critical probabilities are computable numbers with closed forms or intrinsically numerical constants. The problem also stands for a whole family of unknown thresholds in three dimensions, where two-dimensional duality fails and new mathematics is needed.

The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

For site percolation on Z^3 the threshold is approximately 0.3116, also unknown exactly. Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1002
N = 5
P = 0.25


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    grid = [[rng.random() < P for _ in range(N)] for _ in range(N)]
    open_cells = sum(sum(row) for row in grid)
    seen = set()
    stack = [(0, j) for j in range(N) if grid[0][j]]
    while stack:
        x, y = stack.pop()
        if (x, y) in seen:
            continue
        seen.add((x, y))
        for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            if 0 <= nx < N and 0 <= ny < N and grid[nx][ny] and (nx, ny) not in seen:
                stack.append((nx, ny))
    spanning = any((N - 1, j) in seen for j in range(N))
    payload = "perc n=%d p=%s open=%d cluster=%d spanning=%s" % (N, P, open_cells, len(seen), spanning)
    lines = ["Grid: %dx%d, p=%s" % (N, N, P),
             "Open cells: %d" % open_cells,
             "Top cluster size: %d" % len(seen),
             "Spanning cluster: %s" % spanning]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Self-Avoiding Walk Connective Constant in Three Dimensions JAH-USEQPR-000003 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The self-avoiding walk connective constant in three dimensions is the exponential growth rate of the number of self-avoiding walks on the cubic lattice. Let c_n be the number of walks of n steps that never visit a vertex twice. Then c_n grows like mu^n times a power of n, where mu is the connective constant. Numerical estimates put mu at approximately 4.684, but no exact value is known, and the critical exponents that govern the power-law correction are also unknown exactly. The problem asks for the exact constant and exponents in three dimensions, and it is open. The central equation is: mu = lim c_n^{1/n}: what is the exact connective constant of self-avoiding walks on Z^3? Self-avoiding walks are the basic model of a polymer chain in a good solvent: the connective constant controls how many configurations a polymer can adopt, and the exponents control its size and shape. Exact values would give precise predictions for polymer physics, protein folding models and the statistics of long chain molecules. The problem is also a test case for the renormalisation group in three dimensions, where the exponents are believed to be universal but remain uncomputed. Its solution would connect combinatorics, probability and the physics of soft matter.

Statement

The self-avoiding walk connective constant in three dimensions is the exponential growth rate of the number of self-avoiding walks on the cubic lattice. Let c_n be the number of walks of n steps that never visit a vertex twice. Then c_n grows like mu^n times a power of n, where mu is the connective constant. Numerical estimates put mu at approximately 4.684, but no exact value is known, and the critical exponents that govern the power-law correction are also unknown exactly. The problem asks for the exact constant and exponents in three dimensions, and it is open. The equation is: mu = lim c_n^{1/n}: what is the exact connective constant of self-avoiding walks on Z^3?

History

Self-avoiding walks were introduced as a model of polymers in the 1940s and 1950s by Flory and by Hammersley and Morton, who proved that the connective constant exists as a limit. In two dimensions the connective constant for the honeycomb lattice was proved to be exactly the square root of 2 plus the square root of 2 by Duminil-Copin and Smirnov in 2010, a celebrated result using discrete complex analysis. For the square and cubic lattices no exact value is known, and the three-dimensional exponents have been estimated only by enumeration and Monte Carlo simulation.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the connective constant mu exists for every lattice, by subadditivity, and that c_n is bounded between mu^n and a polynomial times mu^n. In three dimensions the best estimates give mu = 4.68404 and the exponents nu = 0.5876 and gamma = 1.1575, from long enumerations and simulations. The mean-square displacement exponent nu is believed to be universal, and the lace expansion proves mean-field behaviour above four dimensions, but in three dimensions no exact values are known. The two-dimensional honeycomb result is the only exact connective constant known for a nontrivial lattice.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000003 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let c_n be the number of self-avoiding walks of length n on Z^3 starting at the origin. The connective constant is mu = lim_{n to infinity} c_n^{1/n}, and the scaling form is c_n ~ A mu^n n^{gamma - 1}. In metamath-style gloss: there exist constants mu, gamma and A such that the ratio c_n / (mu^n n^{gamma - 1}) tends to A as n tends to infinity. The problem asks for the exact real numbers mu and gamma in three dimensions, and for a proof of the scaling form with those values. Enumeration up to dozens of steps supports the form but cannot prove it.

Science Proof

Self-avoiding walks are the basic model of a polymer chain in a good solvent: the connective constant controls how many configurations a polymer can adopt, and the exponents control its size and shape. Exact values would give precise predictions for polymer physics, protein folding models and the statistics of long chain molecules. The problem is also a test case for the renormalisation group in three dimensions, where the exponents are believed to be universal but remain uncomputed. Its solution would connect combinatorics, probability and the physics of soft matter.

Problem

The self-avoiding walk connective constant in three dimensions is the exponential growth rate of the number of self-avoiding walks on the cubic lattice. Let c_n be the number of walks of n steps that never visit a vertex twice. Then c_n grows like mu^n times a power of n, where mu is the connective constant. Numerical estimates put mu at approximately 4.684, but no exact value is known, and the critical exponents that govern the power-law correction are also unknown exactly. The problem asks for the exact constant and exponents in three dimensions, and it is open. In symbols, the problem is: mu = lim c_n^{1/n}: what is the exact connective constant of self-avoiding walks on Z^3? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Working demo program

import hashlib

L = 10


def run():
    count = [0]

    def dfs(x, y, visited, steps):
        if steps == L:
            count[0] += 1
            return
        for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            if (nx, ny) not in visited:
                visited.add((nx, ny))
                dfs(nx, ny, visited, steps + 1)
                visited.remove((nx, ny))

    dfs(0, 0, {(0, 0)}, 0)
    payload = "saw L=%d count=%d" % (L, count[0])
    lines = ["Walk length: %d" % L,
             "Self-avoiding walks: %d" % count[0],
             "Ratio c[L]/c[L-1] is not computed here; only the exact count."]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Critical Exponents of the Three-Dimensional Ising Model JAH-USEQPR-000004 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The critical exponents of the three-dimensional Ising model are unknown exactly. The Ising model places a binary spin at each site of the cubic lattice, with neighbouring spins preferring to align, and at a critical temperature the system undergoes a phase transition between order and disorder. Near that temperature the magnetisation, susceptibility and correlation length follow power laws with exponents beta, gamma and nu. Numerical estimates give nu around 0.630 and beta around 0.326, but no exact values are known, and the problem is one of the great open quantitative questions in statistical physics. The central equation is: What are the exact values of alpha, beta, gamma, nu for the 3D Ising model at criticality? The three-dimensional Ising exponents are the canonical numbers of critical phenomena: they describe magnets, fluids near the liquid-gas critical point, binary alloys and many other systems that share the same universality class. Exact values would confirm the renormalisation group and conformal bootstrap pictures of phase transitions and would give precise predictions for experiments on magnets and fluids. The exponents are also a benchmark for mathematical physics: two dimensions is solved exactly, and three dimensions is the frontier where rigorous mathematics meets high-precision numerics.

Statement

The critical exponents of the three-dimensional Ising model are unknown exactly. The Ising model places a binary spin at each site of the cubic lattice, with neighbouring spins preferring to align, and at a critical temperature the system undergoes a phase transition between order and disorder. Near that temperature the magnetisation, susceptibility and correlation length follow power laws with exponents beta, gamma and nu. Numerical estimates give nu around 0.630 and beta around 0.326, but no exact values are known, and the problem is one of the great open quantitative questions in statistical physics. The equation is: What are the exact values of alpha, beta, gamma, nu for the 3D Ising model at criticality?

History

Lenz and Ising introduced the model in the 1920s, and Onsager solved it exactly in two dimensions in 1944, finding the critical exponents alpha = 0, beta = 1/8 and nu = 1. In three dimensions no exact solution exists, and the exponents have been estimated by high-temperature series, renormalisation group calculations and Monte Carlo simulation since the 1960s. The conformal bootstrap programme of the 2010s, led by Rychkov, Simmons-Duffin and others, has produced the most precise estimates yet, with rigorous error bars, but the exact values remain unknown and the exponents are believed to be irrational numbers with no closed form.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the critical exponents exist and satisfy scaling relations such as alpha + 2 beta + gamma = 2, and the conformal bootstrap gives nu = 0.629971, beta = 0.326419 and gamma = 1.237075, with error bars in the sixth decimal place. The two-dimensional exponents are known exactly from Onsager's solution and conformal field theory. In three dimensions the renormalisation group predicts universality: the same exponents govern every short-range ferromagnetic system in the same class. No exact values are known, and no proof of the scaling relations at the critical point is complete in three dimensions.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000004 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let m(t) be the spontaneous magnetisation at reduced temperature t, chi(t) the susceptibility, and xi(t) the correlation length. The exponents are defined by m ~ |t|^beta, chi ~ |t|^{-gamma} and xi ~ |t|^{-nu} as t tends to zero. In metamath-style gloss: there exist constants beta, gamma, nu in (0, infinity) such that the logarithm of m(t) divided by the logarithm of |t| tends to beta, with analogous limits for chi and xi. The problem asks for the exact real numbers beta, gamma and nu, and for a proof that these limits exist with those values for the cubic lattice Ising model.

Science Proof

The three-dimensional Ising exponents are the canonical numbers of critical phenomena: they describe magnets, fluids near the liquid-gas critical point, binary alloys and many other systems that share the same universality class. Exact values would confirm the renormalisation group and conformal bootstrap pictures of phase transitions and would give precise predictions for experiments on magnets and fluids. The exponents are also a benchmark for mathematical physics: two dimensions is solved exactly, and three dimensions is the frontier where rigorous mathematics meets high-precision numerics.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

Lenz and Ising introduced the model in the 1920s, and Onsager solved it exactly in two dimensions in 1944, finding the critical exponents alpha = 0, beta = 1/8 and nu = 1. In three dimensions no exact solution exists, and the exponents have been estimated by high-temperature series, renormalisation group calculations and Monte Carlo simulation since the 1960s. The conformal bootstrap programme of the 2010s, led by Rychkov, Simmons-Duffin and others, has produced the most precise estimates yet, with rigorous error bars, but the exact values remain unknown and the exponents are believed to be irrational numbers with no closed form. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of probability, statistical physics and random structures, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The critical exponents of the three-dimensional Ising model are unknown exactly. The Ising model places a binary spin at each site of the cubic lattice, with neighbouring spins preferring to align, and at a critical temperature the system undergoes a phase transition between order and disorder. Near that temperature the magnetisation, susceptibility and correlation length follow power laws with exponents beta, gamma and nu. Numerical estimates give nu around 0.630 and beta around 0.326, but no exact values are known, and the problem is one of the great open quantitative questions in statistical physics. In symbols, the problem is: What are the exact values of alpha, beta, gamma, nu for the 3D Ising model at criticality? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from probability, statistical physics and random structures. It is known that the critical exponents exist and satisfy scaling relations such as alpha + 2 beta + gamma = 2, and the conformal bootstrap gives nu = 0.629971, beta = 0.326419 and gamma = 1.237075, with error bars in the sixth decimal place. The two-dimensional exponents are known exactly from Onsager's solution and conformal field theory. In three dimensions the renormalisation group predicts universality: the same exponents govern every short-range ferromagnetic system in the same class. No exact values are known, and no proof of the scaling relations at the critical point is complete in three dimensions. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

The demo is deterministic, so its output can be reproduced byte for byte on any machine with a standard Python installation.

Working demo program

import hashlib

N = 3


def run():
    n = N
    edges = []
    for x in range(n):
        for y in range(n):
            if x + 1 < n:
                edges.append(((x, y), (x + 1, y)))
            if y + 1 < n:
                edges.append(((x, y), (x, y + 1)))
    idx = {(x, y): x * n + y for x in range(n) for y in range(n)}
    best = None
    ground = 0
    total_e = 0
    total_m2 = 0
    configs = 0
    for bits in range(1 << (n * n)):
        s = [1 if (bits >> k) & 1 else -1 for k in range(n * n)]
        e = -sum(s[idx[a]] * s[idx[b]] for a, b in edges)
        m = sum(s)
        total_e += e
        total_m2 += m * m
        configs += 1
        if best is None or e < best:
            best = e
            ground = 1
        elif e == best:
            ground += 1
    payload = "ising n=%d ground_e=%d ground_n=%d mean_e=%.4f" % (n, best, ground, total_e / configs)
    lines = ["Lattice: %dx%d, configurations: %d" % (n, n, configs),
             "Ground-state energy: %d" % best,
             "Ground states: %d" % ground,
             "Mean energy: %.4f" % (total_e / configs),
             "Mean squared magnetisation: %.4f" % (total_m2 / configs)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

KPZ Universality Class JAH-USEQPR-000005 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The KPZ universality class is the conjectured family of one-dimensional growth models that share the same large-scale fluctuations as the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang equation. The equation describes a growing interface h(x, t) with diffusion, a nonlinear slope term and space-time white noise, and its exponents in one space dimension are alpha = 1/2 for roughness, beta = 1/3 for growth and z = 3/2 for dynamics. Many models are believed to lie in this class, but the universality claim is unproven in general: it is known rigorously only for a few exactly solvable models, and the problem is open. The central equation is: h_t = nu h_xx + (lambda/2)(h_x)^2 + eta: do all 1+1 growth models share the exponents alpha=1/2, beta=1/3, z=3/2? The KPZ class governs the growth of crystals, the spread of bacterial colonies, the roughness of burning fronts and the fluctuations of traffic: wherever an interface grows with local randomness, the same exponents are expected. Confirming universality would unify a vast range of phenomena under one mathematical object, the KPZ fixed point, and would give precise predictions for experiments on growing interfaces. The equation is also the prototype of a singular stochastic partial differential equation, so its theory has reshaped the mathematics of randomness itself.

Statement

The KPZ universality class is the conjectured family of one-dimensional growth models that share the same large-scale fluctuations as the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang equation. The equation describes a growing interface h(x, t) with diffusion, a nonlinear slope term and space-time white noise, and its exponents in one space dimension are alpha = 1/2 for roughness, beta = 1/3 for growth and z = 3/2 for dynamics. Many models are believed to lie in this class, but the universality claim is unproven in general: it is known rigorously only for a few exactly solvable models, and the problem is open. The equation is: h_t = nu h_xx + (lambda/2)(h_x)^2 + eta: do all 1+1 growth models share the exponents alpha=1/2, beta=1/3, z=3/2?

History

Kardar, Parisi and Zhang introduced the equation in 1986 to describe growing interfaces, and the exponents were derived by renormalisation group arguments. The totally asymmetric simple exclusion process and related particle systems were shown to have Tracy-Widom fluctuations, confirming the exponents for those models. Hairer won the Fields Medal in 2014 for his theory of regularity structures, which gave the KPZ equation a rigorous meaning, and the KPZ fixed point, constructed by Matetski, Quastel and Remenik in 2017, describes the universal limit for a class of models. But the general universality claim, that every reasonable growth model converges to the fixed point, is unproven.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the exponents alpha = 1/2, beta = 1/3 and z = 3/2 hold for the totally asymmetric simple exclusion process, for last passage percolation and for a handful of other exactly solvable models, with Tracy-Widom limit laws proved. Hairer's regularity structures give a rigorous solution theory for the KPZ equation itself, and the KPZ fixed point is now a well-defined universal object. What is not known is whether an arbitrary growth model in the class, such as the Eden model or ballistic deposition, converges to the fixed point: the universality claim for general models remains conjectural, and even the exponents are unproven for most models in the class.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000005 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the KPZ equation is h_t = nu h_xx + (lambda/2)(h_x)^2 + eta, where eta is space-time white noise, and the universality claim is that the rescaled height fluctuations of a wide class of growth models converge to the KPZ fixed point with exponents alpha = 1/2, beta = 1/3 and z = 3/2. In metamath-style gloss: for all growth models in the KPZ class, the width W(L, t) satisfies W ~ L^alpha f(t / L^z) with alpha = 1/2 and z = 3/2, and the one-point fluctuations converge to the Tracy-Widom distribution. The statement is a universal claim over models, which is why proofs for individual models do not settle it.

Science Proof

The KPZ class governs the growth of crystals, the spread of bacterial colonies, the roughness of burning fronts and the fluctuations of traffic: wherever an interface grows with local randomness, the same exponents are expected. Confirming universality would unify a vast range of phenomena under one mathematical object, the KPZ fixed point, and would give precise predictions for experiments on growing interfaces. The equation is also the prototype of a singular stochastic partial differential equation, so its theory has reshaped the mathematics of randomness itself.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1005
COLS = 12
STEPS = 200


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    h = [0] * COLS
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        h[rng.randrange(COLS)] += 1
    mean = sum(h) / COLS
    width = (sum((v - mean) ** 2 for v in h) / COLS) ** 0.5
    payload = "growth cols=%d steps=%d width=%.4f maxh=%d" % (COLS, STEPS, width, max(h))
    lines = ["Columns: %d, deposition steps: %d" % (COLS, STEPS),
             "Mean height: %.4f" % mean,
             "Interface width: %.4f" % width,
             "Maximum height: %d" % max(h)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

SLE Convergence for Square-Lattice Critical Percolation JAH-USEQPR-000006 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The convergence of critical percolation interfaces on the square lattice to Schramm-Loewner evolution with parameter 6 is an open problem. For site percolation on the triangular lattice, Smirnov proved in 2001 that the interfaces converge to SLE(6), confirming the conformal invariance predicted by physicists. For the square lattice Z^2, which is the most natural lattice for applications, no such proof exists: the discrete complex analysis that works on the triangular lattice does not carry over, and the scaling limit is unproven. The problem asks for a proof that square-lattice critical percolation has SLE(6) as its scaling limit. The central equation is: Does the scaling limit of critical percolation interfaces on Z^2 converge to SLE(6)? Conformal invariance of square-lattice percolation would complete the two-dimensional theory of critical phenomena for the most common lattice, and would confirm that the continuum limit does not depend on the microscopic lattice. It would also validate the use of SLE as a universal tool for planar random curves, with applications to polymers, domain walls in magnets and the geometry of random interfaces. The problem is a benchmark for the methods of discrete complex analysis: solving it would likely require a new idea that extends Smirnov's proof beyond the triangular lattice.

Statement

The convergence of critical percolation interfaces on the square lattice to Schramm-Loewner evolution with parameter 6 is an open problem. For site percolation on the triangular lattice, Smirnov proved in 2001 that the interfaces converge to SLE(6), confirming the conformal invariance predicted by physicists. For the square lattice Z^2, which is the most natural lattice for applications, no such proof exists: the discrete complex analysis that works on the triangular lattice does not carry over, and the scaling limit is unproven. The problem asks for a proof that square-lattice critical percolation has SLE(6) as its scaling limit. The equation is: Does the scaling limit of critical percolation interfaces on Z^2 converge to SLE(6)?

History

Oded Schramm introduced SLE in 2000 as the conjectured scaling limit of critical interfaces, and Smirnov proved the triangular-lattice case in 2001 using Cardy's formula and discrete holomorphicity. The result earned Smirnov the Fields Medal in 2010. For the square lattice, the Russo-Seymour-Welsh estimates give crossing probabilities, and the existence of a scaling limit is plausible, but the conformal invariance and the identification of the limit as SLE(6) remain unproven. The problem has resisted the methods that worked on the triangular lattice, because the square lattice lacks the exact discrete harmonicity that made the triangular case tractable.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that site percolation on the triangular lattice converges to SLE(6), by Smirnov's theorem, and that the critical exponents in two dimensions are known exactly through SLE and conformal field theory. For the square lattice, crossing probabilities are known to be bounded away from zero and one by Russo-Seymour-Welsh theory, and the one-arm exponent is known to be 5/48 by the work of Lawler, Schramm and Werner, which used the triangular lattice result. What is not known is the conformal invariance of the square-lattice interfaces or their convergence to SLE(6); the scaling limit of the square lattice remains conjectural.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000006 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let gamma_n be the interface of critical bond percolation on the square lattice at mesh size 1/n, and let SLE(6) be the Schramm-Loewner evolution with parameter kappa = 6. The conjecture is that gamma_n converges in distribution to SLE(6) as n tends to infinity. In metamath-style gloss: for all bounded continuous test functions F on curves, the expectation of F(gamma_n) tends to the expectation of F under SLE(6). The statement is a convergence claim for random curves, and proving it requires controlling the discrete interfaces at all scales simultaneously.

Science Proof

Conformal invariance of square-lattice percolation would complete the two-dimensional theory of critical phenomena for the most common lattice, and would confirm that the continuum limit does not depend on the microscopic lattice. It would also validate the use of SLE as a universal tool for planar random curves, with applications to polymers, domain walls in magnets and the geometry of random interfaces. The problem is a benchmark for the methods of discrete complex analysis: solving it would likely require a new idea that extends Smirnov's proof beyond the triangular lattice.

Problem

The convergence of critical percolation interfaces on the square lattice to Schramm-Loewner evolution with parameter 6 is an open problem. A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1006
N = 6
P = 0.5


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    grid = [[rng.random() < P for _ in range(N)] for _ in range(N)]
    open_cells = sum(sum(row) for row in grid)
    seen = set()
    stack = [(0, j) for j in range(N) if grid[0][j]]
    while stack:
        x, y = stack.pop()
        if (x, y) in seen:
            continue
        seen.add((x, y))
        for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            if 0 <= nx < N and 0 <= ny < N and grid[nx][ny] and (nx, ny) not in seen:
                stack.append((nx, ny))
    spanning = any((N - 1, j) in seen for j in range(N))
    payload = "perc n=%d p=%s open=%d cluster=%d spanning=%s" % (N, P, open_cells, len(seen), spanning)
    lines = ["Grid: %dx%d, p=%s" % (N, N, P),
             "Open cells: %d" % open_cells,
             "Top cluster size: %d" % len(seen),
             "Spanning cluster: %s" % spanning]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Random 3-SAT Satisfiability Threshold JAH-USEQPR-000007 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The random 3-SAT satisfiability threshold problem asks for the exact location of the phase transition in random 3-SAT. Take a random formula with n variables and m = r n clauses, each clause a random disjunction of three literals. As r increases, the probability that the formula is satisfiable drops sharply from near one to near zero. Friedgut proved that a threshold sequence r_c(n) exists, but it is not known whether it converges to a constant, and its value is conjectured by physicists to be about 4.267. The exact threshold is open, and the problem is one of the central questions at the meeting point of probability, combinatorics and computer science. The central equation is: Does r_c(n) converge to a constant c_3, and what is its exact value (conjectured about 4.267)? The 3-SAT threshold is the prototype of a phase transition in computation: it explains why random instances are easy far from the threshold and hard near it, and it governs the performance of SAT solvers that underpin hardware verification, planning and artificial intelligence. Its exact value would confirm the cavity method of statistical physics as a rigorous tool and would complete the theory of random constraint satisfaction. The problem also connects probability, combinatorics and computer science, and its solution would likely introduce methods applicable to many other random structures.

Statement

The random 3-SAT satisfiability threshold problem asks for the exact location of the phase transition in random 3-SAT. Take a random formula with n variables and m = r n clauses, each clause a random disjunction of three literals. As r increases, the probability that the formula is satisfiable drops sharply from near one to near zero. Friedgut proved that a threshold sequence r_c(n) exists, but it is not known whether it converges to a constant, and its value is conjectured by physicists to be about 4.267. The exact threshold is open, and the problem is one of the central questions at the meeting point of probability, combinatorics and computer science. The equation is: Does r_c(n) converge to a constant c_3, and what is its exact value (conjectured about 4.267)?

History

The phase transition in random satisfiability was observed experimentally by Cheeseman, Kanefsky and Taylor in 1991 and by Mitchell, Selman and Levesque in 1992, who found the sharp drop in satisfiability around ratio 4.25. Friedgut proved in 1999 that a sharp threshold exists, and Achlioptas and others proved the threshold for large k. The physics approach of Mezard, Parisi and Zecchina used the cavity method to predict the value 4.267 in 2002, and Ding, Sly and Sun proved in 2015 that the threshold exists for large k, but the case k = 3, the most studied, remains open: neither convergence of the threshold sequence nor its exact value is known.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that a sharp threshold sequence r_c(n) exists for random 3-SAT, by Friedgut's theorem, and that the threshold is bounded between 3.52 and 4.506 by rigorous results. For large k, Ding, Sly and Sun proved that the threshold converges to a constant given by an explicit formula, settling the large-k case. The conjectured value for k = 3 is about 4.267, from the cavity method, and simulations agree. What is not known is whether r_c(n) converges at all for k = 3, or what its limit is if it does: the problem is open in the most basic sense, and the rigorous bounds remain far from the conjectured value.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000007 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let F(n, m) be a uniformly random 3-CNF formula with n variables and m clauses, and let r = m/n. The threshold question is whether there is a constant c_3 such that for r < c_3 the formula is satisfiable with probability tending to one, and for r > c_3 it is unsatisfiable with probability tending to one. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a real number c_3 such that for all eps > 0, the satisfiability probability tends to one when r = c_3 - eps and to zero when r = c_3 + eps. Friedgut's theorem gives only a sequence r_c(n), and the problem asks whether the sequence converges and to what.

Science Proof

The 3-SAT threshold is the prototype of a phase transition in computation: it explains why random instances are easy far from the threshold and hard near it, and it governs the performance of SAT solvers that underpin hardware verification, planning and artificial intelligence. Its exact value would confirm the cavity method of statistical physics as a rigorous tool and would complete the theory of random constraint satisfaction. The problem also connects probability, combinatorics and computer science, and its solution would likely introduce methods applicable to many other random structures.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools
import random

SEED = 1007
NVAR = 6
NCLAUSES = 18
TRIALS = 12


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    sat_count = 0
    for _ in range(TRIALS):
        clauses = []
        for _ in range(NCLAUSES):
            vs = rng.sample(range(NVAR), 3)
            clauses.append([(v, rng.random() < 0.5) for v in vs])
        found = False
        for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
            if all(any((vals[v] if pos else not vals[v]) for v, pos in cl) for cl in clauses):
                found = True
                break
        if found:
            sat_count += 1
    payload = "sat3 nvar=%d trials=%d sat=%d" % (NVAR, TRIALS, sat_count)
    lines = ["Variables: %d, clauses per formula: %d" % (NVAR, NCLAUSES),
             "Random formulas tried: %d" % TRIALS,
             "Satisfiable formulas: %d" % sat_count]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")

Full working program

import hashlib
import itertools
import random

SEED = 1007
NVAR = 6
NCLAUSES = 18
TRIALS = 12


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    sat_count = 0
    for _ in range(TRIALS):
        clauses = []
        for _ in range(NCLAUSES):
            vs = rng.sample(range(NVAR), 3)
            clauses.append([(v, rng.random() < 0.5) for v in vs])
        found = False
        for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
            if all(any((vals[v] if pos else not vals[v]) for v, pos in cl) for cl in clauses):
                found = True
                break
        if found:
            sat_count += 1
    payload = "sat3 nvar=%d trials=%d sat=%d" % (NVAR, TRIALS, sat_count)
    lines = ["Variables: %d, clauses per formula: %d" % (NVAR, NCLAUSES),
             "Random formulas tried: %d" % TRIALS,
             "Satisfiable formulas: %d" % sat_count]
    return payload, lines


print("Record: Random 3-SAT Satisfiability Threshold")
print("Status: OPEN")
print("Statement: " + 'The random 3-SAT satisfiability threshold problem asks for the exact location of the phase transition in random 3-SAT. As r increases, the probability that the formula is satisfiable drops sharply from near one to near zero. Friedgut proved that a threshold sequence r_c(n) exists, but it')
print("Known: " + "It is known that a sharp threshold sequence r_c(n) exists for random 3-SAT, by Friedgut's theorem, and that the threshold is bounded between 3.52 and 4.506 by rigorous results. For large k, Ding, Sly and Sun proved that the threshold converges to a constant given by an explicit formula, settling the large-k case. The conjectured value for k = 3 is about 4.267, from the cavity method, and simulatio")
print("This public record carries no solving attempt.")

    print("Verification (same computation as the demo):")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    print("Program complete.")

Chromatic Number of Random Graphs JAH-USEQPR-000008 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The chromatic number of a random graph is the smallest number of colours needed to colour its vertices so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour. For the random graph G(n, 1/2), where each pair of vertices is joined independently with probability one half, the chromatic number is known to be about n divided by twice the logarithm of n, but the exact asymptotic constant and the precise fluctuations are unknown. The problem asks for the exact asymptotic value: whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value, and what that value is, as n grows. The central equation is: chi(G(n, 1/2)) = ? (what is the exact asymptotic value of the chromatic number of a random graph?) The chromatic number of a random graph is the benchmark for graph colouring: it measures how hard it is to schedule, allocate frequencies, register variables and colour maps in the average case. Exact asymptotics would give precise predictions for the performance of colouring algorithms and would complete the theory of random graphs, one of the most developed areas of combinatorics. The problem also tests the methods of probabilistic combinatorics, where first-order asymptotics are often easy and second-order terms are hard. Its solution would be a landmark in the field.

Statement

The chromatic number of a random graph is the smallest number of colours needed to colour its vertices so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour. For the random graph G(n, 1/2), where each pair of vertices is joined independently with probability one half, the chromatic number is known to be about n divided by twice the logarithm of n, but the exact asymptotic constant and the precise fluctuations are unknown. The problem asks for the exact asymptotic value: whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value, and what that value is, as n grows. The equation is: chi(G(n, 1/2)) = ? (what is the exact asymptotic value of the chromatic number of a random graph?)

History

Erdos and Renyi introduced random graphs in 1959, and Bollobas determined the asymptotic order of the chromatic number in 1988, showing it is (1 + o(1)) n / (2 log_2 n). The finer question of the exact constant resisted progress for decades. Alon and Krivelevich showed in the 1990s that the chromatic number is concentrated on a few values, and recent work by Heckel and others has narrowed the concentration interval. But the precise asymptotic value, including the second-order term, remains unknown, and the problem is one of the oldest open questions in random graph theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the chromatic number of G(n, 1/2) is (1 + o(1)) n / (2 log_2 n), by Bollobas's theorem, and that it is concentrated on at most a few consecutive integers, by the work of Alon, Krivelevich and others. The independence number, which controls the chromatic number from below, is known to be about 2 log_2 n, and the greedy colouring algorithm achieves a colouring within a constant factor of optimal. What is not known is the exact second-order term of the chromatic number, or whether it concentrates on a single value: the interval of possible values has been narrowed but not closed, and the precise asymptotics remain open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000008 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let chi(G) be the chromatic number of a graph G, and let G(n, 1/2) be the random graph on n vertices with edge probability one half. The problem asks for the exact asymptotic expansion of chi(G(n, 1/2)), in particular for the constant c in chi ~ n / (2 log_2 n) + c n / (log n)^2, and for the width of the concentration interval. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function f(n) such that chi(G(n, 1/2)) / f(n) tends to one in probability, and the problem asks for the exact function f, including its second-order term. The statement is a claim about the precise asymptotics of a random variable.

Science Proof

The chromatic number of a random graph is the benchmark for graph colouring: it measures how hard it is to schedule, allocate frequencies, register variables and colour maps in the average case. Exact asymptotics would give precise predictions for the performance of colouring algorithms and would complete the theory of random graphs, one of the most developed areas of combinatorics. The problem also tests the methods of probabilistic combinatorics, where first-order asymptotics are often easy and second-order terms are hard. Its solution would be a landmark in the field.

Problem

The chromatic number of a random graph is the smallest number of colours needed to colour its vertices so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour. For the random graph G(n, 1/2), where each pair of vertices is joined independently with probability one half, the chromatic number is known to be about n divided by twice the logarithm of n, but the exact asymptotic constant and the precise fluctuations are unknown. The problem asks for the exact asymptotic value: whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value, and what that value is, as n grows. In symbols, the problem is: chi(G(n, 1/2)) = ? (what is the exact asymptotic value of the chromatic number of a random graph?) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1008
N = 8
P = 0.5


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    adj = {i: set() for i in range(N)}
    edges = 0
    for i in range(N):
        for j in range(i + 1, N):
            if rng.random() < P:
                adj[i].add(j)
                adj[j].add(i)
                edges += 1
    greedy = 0
    colour = {}
    for v in range(N):
        used = {colour[u] for u in adj[v] if u in colour}
        c = 0
        while c in used:
            c += 1
        colour[v] = c
        greedy = max(greedy, c + 1)
    order = sorted(range(N), key=lambda v: -len(adj[v]))
    best = [N]

    def search(k, col, used_max):
        if used_max >= best[0]:
            return
        if k == N:
            best[0] = used_max
            return
        v = order[k]
        taken = {col[u] for u in adj[v] if u in col}
        for c in range(used_max + 1):
            if c not in taken:
                col[v] = c
                search(k + 1, col, max(used_max, c + 1))
                del col[v]
        return

    search(0, {}, 0)
    payload = "rgraph n=%d edges=%d chi=%d greedy=%d" % (N, edges, best[0], greedy)
    lines = ["Vertices: %d, edges: %d" % (N, edges),
             "Exact chromatic number: %d" % best[0],
             "Greedy colouring uses: %d" % greedy]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Directed Percolation Critical Exponents JAH-USEQPR-000009 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

Directed percolation is the model of a fluid flowing under gravity, or an infection spreading forward in time: bonds are open with probability p, but paths must respect a preferred direction. At a critical probability p_c the system undergoes a phase transition, and near it the survival probability and correlation lengths follow power laws with critical exponents. The exponents are known numerically, with beta around 0.277 in one space dimension, but no exact values are known in any dimension, and directed percolation is famous as the simplest model whose exponents resist exact solution. The problem asks for the exact exponents. The central equation is: What are the exact critical exponents of directed percolation in 1+1 and higher dimensions? Directed percolation exponents govern epidemics spreading forward in time, the flow of water through soil, the spread of forest fires and the behaviour of catalytic reactions: every system with an absorbing state and a preferred direction shares these numbers. Exact values would confirm the Janssen-Grassberger universality hypothesis and would give precise predictions for non-equilibrium systems, which are far less understood than equilibrium ones. The problem is also a test of the renormalisation group outside equilibrium, where the methods are less developed and the need for exact results is greatest.

Statement

Directed percolation is the model of a fluid flowing under gravity, or an infection spreading forward in time: bonds are open with probability p, but paths must respect a preferred direction. At a critical probability p_c the system undergoes a phase transition, and near it the survival probability and correlation lengths follow power laws with critical exponents. The exponents are known numerically, with beta around 0.277 in one space dimension, but no exact values are known in any dimension, and directed percolation is famous as the simplest model whose exponents resist exact solution. The problem asks for the exact exponents. The equation is: What are the exact critical exponents of directed percolation in 1+1 and higher dimensions?

History

Broadbent and Hammersley introduced directed percolation in 1957 alongside ordinary percolation. The model became central in the 1980s when it was recognised as the prototype of a non-equilibrium phase transition, with applications to epidemics, forest fires and fluid flow. The Janssen-Grassberger conjecture of the 1980s asserts that every model with a single absorbing state and short-range interactions lies in the directed percolation class, making its exponents universal. Despite decades of simulation, series expansion and renormalisation group work, no exact exponent has been derived, and the class remains without an exactly solved representative.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that directed percolation has a sharp phase transition with a critical probability p_c, and that the exponents satisfy scaling relations, by the work of Durrett and others on the contact process, which is in the same class. Numerical estimates give beta = 0.2765, nu_parallel = 1.7338 and nu_perp = 1.0968 in one space dimension, with small error bars. The Janssen-Grassberger conjecture is supported by extensive simulation but unproven. No exact values are known for the exponents in any dimension, and no conformal field theory or exact solution exists for the class, which is why directed percolation is called the most important unsolved problem in non-equilibrium statistical physics.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000009 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let theta(p) be the probability that a directed path from the origin survives forever, and let xi_parallel and xi_perp be the correlation lengths along and across the preferred direction. The exponents are defined by theta(p) ~ (p - p_c)^beta and xi ~ |p - p_c|^{-nu} as p approaches p_c. In metamath-style gloss: there exist constants beta, nu_parallel and nu_perp such that the logarithms of these quantities divided by the logarithm of |p - p_c| tend to the respective exponents. The problem asks for the exact real numbers beta, nu_parallel and nu_perp in one space dimension and above.

Science Proof

Directed percolation exponents govern epidemics spreading forward in time, the flow of water through soil, the spread of forest fires and the behaviour of catalytic reactions: every system with an absorbing state and a preferred direction shares these numbers. Exact values would confirm the Janssen-Grassberger universality hypothesis and would give precise predictions for non-equilibrium systems, which are far less understood than equilibrium ones. The problem is also a test of the renormalisation group outside equilibrium, where the methods are less developed and the need for exact results is greatest.

The problem asks for the exact exponents. In symbols, the problem is: What are the exact critical exponents of directed percolation in 1+1 and higher dimensions? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from probability, statistical physics and random structures. It is known that directed percolation has a sharp phase transition with a critical probability p_c, and that the exponents satisfy scaling relations, by the work of Durrett and others on the contact process, which is in the same class. Numerical estimates give beta = 0.2765, nu_parallel = 1.7338 and nu_perp = 1.0968 in one space dimension, with small error bars. The Janssen-Grassberger conjecture is supported by extensive simulation but unproven. No exact values are known for the exponents in any dimension, and no conformal field theory or exact solution exists for the class, which is why directed percolation is called the most important unsolved problem in non-equilibrium statistical physics. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1009
WIDTH = 10
STEPS = 12
P = 0.7


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    active = {WIDTH // 2}
    survived = 0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        nxt = set()
        for x in active:
            for dx in (-1, 0, 1):
                if rng.random() < P:
                    nxt.add(x + dx)
        active = {x for x in nxt if 0 <= x < WIDTH}
        if not active:
            break
        survived += 1
    payload = "dperc width=%d steps=%d survived=%d final=%d" % (WIDTH, STEPS, survived, len(active))
    lines = ["Width: %d, time steps: %d, p=%s" % (WIDTH, STEPS, P),
             "Steps survived: %d" % survived,
             "Active sites at end: %d" % len(active)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
