Eden Model Fluctuations JAH-USEQPR-000019 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The Eden model is the simplest growth model: start with a single occupied cell, and repeatedly add a random cell from the boundary of the cluster. The cluster grows roughly like a ball, but its boundary fluctuates, and the size of the fluctuations is unknown rigorously. The model is believed to lie in the KPZ universality class, with fluctuations of order t^{1/3} in two dimensions, but no proof exists, and even the limit shape of the cluster is not known exactly: it is believed to be a disc in two dimensions, but the shape is unproven. The problem asks for the fluctuations and the limit shape of the Eden model. The central equation is: Do Eden model clusters have KPZ fluctuations with exponent 1/3, and what is their limit shape? The Eden model describes the growth of bacterial colonies, tumors and crystals: its fluctuations determine how rough the growing front is, which matters for the biology of colonies and the physics of interfaces. A proof of the KPZ exponent would confirm the universality class for the simplest possible model and would validate the whole KPZ programme. The limit shape question is equally basic: it asks for the asymptotic form of a growing blob, one of the simplest questions in random growth. The problem is therefore a fundamental test of the theory of growing interfaces.

Statement

The Eden model is the simplest growth model: start with a single occupied cell, and repeatedly add a random cell from the boundary of the cluster. The cluster grows roughly like a ball, but its boundary fluctuates, and the size of the fluctuations is unknown rigorously. The model is believed to lie in the KPZ universality class, with fluctuations of order t^{1/3} in two dimensions, but no proof exists, and even the limit shape of the cluster is not known exactly: it is believed to be a disc in two dimensions, but the shape is unproven. The problem asks for the fluctuations and the limit shape of the Eden model. The equation is: Do Eden model clusters have KPZ fluctuations with exponent 1/3, and what is their limit shape?

History

Murray Eden introduced the model in 1961 to describe the growth of bacterial colonies and tumors. The model became a prototype of random growth, and Richardson proved in 1973 that the rescaled cluster converges to a deterministic shape, but the shape was not identified. The KPZ equation, introduced in 1986, predicted the fluctuation exponent 1/3 for models in its class, and the Eden model was conjectured to belong to it. Since then the exponent has been confirmed by simulation, but no rigorous proof exists, and the limit shape is known only to be convex and symmetric, not to be a disc.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Eden model has a deterministic limit shape, by Richardson's shape theorem, and that the shape is convex and has the symmetries of the lattice. Simulations give a fluctuation exponent of about 1/3 in two dimensions, consistent with the KPZ class, and the one-point fluctuations are believed to follow the Tracy-Widom distribution. What is not known is a proof of the exponent, a proof that the limit shape is a disc, or a proof that the model converges to the KPZ fixed point: the Eden model is the simplest growth model, and yet its fluctuations remain unproven, which shows how hard the KPZ universality problem is.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000019 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let E_t be the Eden cluster at time t, and let R_t be its radius. The shape theorem says that E_t / t converges to a deterministic convex set, and the fluctuation question asks whether the boundary fluctuations are of order t^{1/3} with Tracy-Widom statistics. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a convex set S and an exponent beta = 1/3 such that the rescaled cluster converges to S and the rescaled boundary fluctuations converge to the Tracy-Widom distribution, and the problem asks for a proof of this statement and the identification of S. The statement is a claim about the scaling of a random growth process.

Science Proof

The Eden model describes the growth of bacterial colonies, tumors and crystals: its fluctuations determine how rough the growing front is, which matters for the biology of colonies and the physics of interfaces. A proof of the KPZ exponent would confirm the universality class for the simplest possible model and would validate the whole KPZ programme. The limit shape question is equally basic: it asks for the asymptotic form of a growing blob, one of the simplest questions in random growth. The problem is therefore a fundamental test of the theory of growing interfaces.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1019
STEPS = 15
SIZE = 15


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    c = SIZE // 2
    cluster = {(c, c)}
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        boundary = set()
        for (x, y) in cluster:
            for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
                nb = (x + dx, y + dy)
                if nb not in cluster and 0 <= nb[0] < SIZE and 0 <= nb[1] < SIZE:
                    boundary.add(nb)
        cluster.add(rng.choice(sorted(boundary)))
    radius = max(abs(x - c) + abs(y - c) for x, y in cluster)
    payload = "eden steps=%d size=%d radius=%d" % (STEPS, len(cluster), radius)
    lines = ["Growth steps: %d" % STEPS,
             "Cluster size: %d" % len(cluster),
             "Cluster radius (Manhattan): %d" % radius]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Full working program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1019
STEPS = 15
SIZE = 15


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    c = SIZE // 2
    cluster = {(c, c)}
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        boundary = set()
        for (x, y) in cluster:
            for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
                nb = (x + dx, y + dy)
                if nb not in cluster and 0 <= nb[0] < SIZE and 0 <= nb[1] < SIZE:
                    boundary.add(nb)
        cluster.add(rng.choice(sorted(boundary)))
    radius = max(abs(x - c) + abs(y - c) for x, y in cluster)
    payload = "eden steps=%d size=%d radius=%d" % (STEPS, len(cluster), radius)
    lines = ["Growth steps: %d" % STEPS,
             "Cluster size: %d" % len(cluster),
             "Cluster radius (Manhattan): %d" % radius]
    return payload, lines


print("Record: Eden Model Fluctuations")
print("Status: OPEN")
print("Statement: " + 'The Eden model is the simplest growth model: start with a single occupied cell, and repeatedly add a random cell from the boundary of the cluster. The cluster grows roughly like a ball, but its boundary fluctuates, and the size of the fluctuations is unknown rigorously. The model is believed to lie in the KPZ universality class, with fluctuations of order t^{1/3} in two dimensions, but no proof ex')
print("Known: " + "It is known that the Eden model has a deterministic limit shape, by Richardson's shape theorem, and that the shape is convex and has the symmetries of the lattice. Simulations give a fluctuation exponent of about 1/3 in two dimensions, consistent with the KPZ class, and the one-point fluctuations are believed to follow the Tracy-Widom distribution. What is not known is a proof of the exponent, a p")

Non-Hermitian Random Matrix Universality JAH-USEQPR-000020 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

Non-Hermitian random matrix universality asks whether the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of large random matrices without symmetry follow the same laws regardless of the distribution of the entries. For Hermitian matrices the universality is largely proved: the bulk statistics follow the sine kernel and the edge follows the Tracy-Widom law. For non-Hermitian matrices the circular law, which says the eigenvalues fill a disc uniformly, is proved, but the local statistics at the edge, the eigenvector overlaps and the behaviour of the smallest singular values are open in general. The problem asks for the universal laws of non-Hermitian random matrices. The central equation is: Do the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of non-Hermitian random matrices follow universal laws at the edge and in the bulk? Non-Hermitian random matrices model open quantum systems, neural networks, ecological communities and the stability of complex systems: their eigenvalues determine whether a system is stable, and their eigenvectors determine how it responds to perturbations. Universal laws would give precise predictions for the stability of large networks and the spectra of large data matrices, which are used in machine learning and statistics. The problem is also a frontier of probability, where the Hermitian theory is mature and the non-Hermitian theory is still being built, with the edge as the hardest case.

Statement

Non-Hermitian random matrix universality asks whether the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of large random matrices without symmetry follow the same laws regardless of the distribution of the entries. For Hermitian matrices the universality is largely proved: the bulk statistics follow the sine kernel and the edge follows the Tracy-Widom law. For non-Hermitian matrices the circular law, which says the eigenvalues fill a disc uniformly, is proved, but the local statistics at the edge, the eigenvector overlaps and the behaviour of the smallest singular values are open in general. The problem asks for the universal laws of non-Hermitian random matrices. The equation is: Do the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of non-Hermitian random matrices follow universal laws at the edge and in the bulk?

History

Wigner introduced random matrices in the 1950s to model the spectra of heavy nuclei, and the circular law for non-Hermitian matrices was conjectured by Ginibre in 1965. The law was proved for matrices with independent entries by Tao and Vu in 2008, after decades of work. The local statistics of Hermitian matrices were proved universal by Erdos, Schlein, Yau and others in the 2000s, using the local semicircle law and Dyson Brownian motion. For non-Hermitian matrices the corresponding local laws are far less developed, because the eigenvalues are complex and the resolvent is harder to control, and the edge statistics remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the empirical spectral distribution of a large non-Hermitian random matrix with independent entries converges to the uniform distribution on the disc, by the circular law of Tao and Vu, and that the local statistics in the bulk are universal for matrices with a small Gaussian component, by recent work on the local circular law. The eigenvector overlaps, the condition numbers and the edge fluctuations are understood only in special cases, such as the Ginibre ensemble, where exact formulas exist. What is not known is the universality of the edge statistics and eigenvector statistics for general entry distributions, or the precise behaviour of the smallest singular value.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000020 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let M_n be an n by n random matrix with independent entries of mean zero and variance 1/n, and let lambda_i be its eigenvalues. The circular law says that the empirical distribution of the lambda_i converges to the uniform distribution on the unit disc. In metamath-style gloss: for all test functions f, the average of f(lambda_i) converges to the integral of f over the disc, and the universality question asks whether the local correlations of the lambda_i, rescaled at the edge and in the bulk, converge to universal limits independent of the entry distribution. The statement is a claim about the fine structure of random spectra.

Science Proof

Non-Hermitian random matrices model open quantum systems, neural networks, ecological communities and the stability of complex systems: their eigenvalues determine whether a system is stable, and their eigenvectors determine how it responds to perturbations. Universal laws would give precise predictions for the stability of large networks and the spectra of large data matrices, which are used in machine learning and statistics. The problem is also a frontier of probability, where the Hermitian theory is mature and the non-Hermitian theory is still being built, with the edge as the hardest case.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1020
N = 4


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    m = [[rng.uniform(-1.0, 1.0) for _ in range(N)] for _ in range(N)]
    frob = sum(v * v for row in m for v in row) ** 0.5
    v = [1.0] * N
    for _ in range(60):
        w = [sum(m[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(N)) for i in range(N)]
        w2 = [sum(m[j][i] * w[j] for j in range(N)) for i in range(N)]
        nrm = sum(x * x for x in w2) ** 0.5
        if nrm == 0:
            break
        v = [x / nrm for x in w2]
    mv = [sum(m[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(N)) for i in range(N)]
    sig = sum(x * x for x in mv) ** 0.5
    tr = sum(m[i][i] for i in range(N))
    payload = "rmat n=%d frob=%.4f sigma1=%.4f trace=%.4f" % (N, frob, sig, tr)
    lines = ["Matrix size: %dx%d (seeded, entries uniform(-1,1))" % (N, N),
             "Frobenius norm: %.4f" % frob,
             "Largest singular value (power iteration): %.4f" % sig,
             "Trace: %.4f" % tr]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Random Walks in Random Environment in Dimension Two and Above JAH-USEQPR-000021 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

A random walk in a random environment moves on the lattice with transition probabilities that are themselves random, chosen once and fixed. In one dimension the behaviour is understood: the walk can be sub-ballistic and have strange limit laws, by the work of Solomon and Sinai. In two dimensions and above the walk is believed to be ballistic, moving with a positive velocity in a random direction, with Gaussian fluctuations, but no general proof exists. The problem asks whether the walk is ballistic in dimensions two and above, what its velocity is, and whether the central limit theorem holds. The central equation is: Are random walks in random environment in d >= 2 ballistic with a Gaussian limit, and what is the velocity? Random walks in random environment model the motion of particles through disordered media, the spread of populations in heterogeneous landscapes, and the transport of charge in random materials: the velocity and fluctuations determine how fast the system moves and how it spreads. A proof of ballisticity would confirm the picture of transport in random media and would give precise predictions for diffusion in disordered systems. The problem is also a test case for the methods of probability in random media, where one dimension is solved and higher dimensions remain mysterious.

Statement

A random walk in a random environment moves on the lattice with transition probabilities that are themselves random, chosen once and fixed. In one dimension the behaviour is understood: the walk can be sub-ballistic and have strange limit laws, by the work of Solomon and Sinai. In two dimensions and above the walk is believed to be ballistic, moving with a positive velocity in a random direction, with Gaussian fluctuations, but no general proof exists. The problem asks whether the walk is ballistic in dimensions two and above, what its velocity is, and whether the central limit theorem holds. The equation is: Are random walks in random environment in d >= 2 ballistic with a Gaussian limit, and what is the velocity?

History

Random walks in random environment were introduced by Solomon in 1975, who classified the one-dimensional behaviour, and Sinai proved in 1982 that the one-dimensional walk is logarithmically slow, with a famous arcsine-type limit law. In higher dimensions the problem is far harder: the environment is a random field of transition probabilities, and the walk must navigate it without getting trapped. Sznitman developed the theory of ballisticity conditions in the 2000s, proving that under a certain condition the walk is ballistic with a Gaussian limit, but the condition itself is not known to hold for the standard models, such as uniformly elliptic environments with a small drift.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the one-dimensional walk is fully understood, with Solomon's classification and Sinai's limit law, and that in higher dimensions the walk is ballistic under Sznitman's condition (T), which implies a central limit theorem. It is also known that the walk is transient in dimensions two and above under mild assumptions, by the work of Kalikow and others. What is not known is whether condition (T) holds for the standard uniformly elliptic models, or whether the walk is ballistic in general in dimensions two and above: the problem is open, and the velocity of the walk is unknown even in the simplest models.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000021 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let omega be a random environment assigning transition probabilities omega(x, e) at each site x, and let X_n be the walk in that environment. The ballisticity question asks whether the limit of X_n / n exists and is non-zero, and whether (X_n - n v) / sqrt(n) converges to a Gaussian. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a velocity vector v and a covariance matrix Sigma such that X_n / n tends to v almost surely and the rescaled walk converges to a Gaussian with covariance Sigma, and the problem asks for a proof of this statement and the value of v for the standard models in dimensions two and above.

Science Proof

Random walks in random environment model the motion of particles through disordered media, the spread of populations in heterogeneous landscapes, and the transport of charge in random materials: the velocity and fluctuations determine how fast the system moves and how it spreads. A proof of ballisticity would confirm the picture of transport in random media and would give precise predictions for diffusion in disordered systems. The problem is also a test case for the methods of probability in random media, where one dimension is solved and higher dimensions remain mysterious.

No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the problem be stated in the language of probability, statistical physics and random structures. The statement is: Are random walks in random environment in d >= 2 ballistic with a Gaussian limit, and what is the velocity? The gloss is a formal rendering of the informal statement: it quantifies over the objects the problem concerns, it states the required property exactly, and it leaves no room for a reading under which a partial result would count as a solution. Proof sketch

It is known that the one-dimensional walk is fully understood, with Solomon's classification and Sinai's limit law, and that in higher dimensions the walk is ballistic under Sznitman's condition (T), which implies a central limit theorem. It is also known that the walk is transient in dimensions two and above under mild assumptions, by the work of Kalikow and others. What is not known is whether condition (T) holds for the standard uniformly elliptic models, or whether the walk is ballistic in general in dimensions two and above: the problem is open, and the velocity of the walk is unknown even in the simplest models. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1021
STEPS = 20


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    env = {}
    x = 0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        if x not in env:
            env[x] = rng.uniform(0.3, 0.7)
        x += 1 if rng.random() < env[x] else -1
    payload = "rwre steps=%d final=%d sites=%d" % (STEPS, x, len(env))
    lines = ["Walk steps: %d" % STEPS,
             "Final position: %d" % x,
             "Distinct sites visited: %d" % len(env)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Abelian Sandpile Scaling Limit JAH-USEQPR-000022 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The abelian sandpile model is a cellular automaton in which grains of sand are added to a lattice and any site with too many grains topples, sending one grain to each neighbour. The model exhibits self-organised criticality: it drives itself to a critical state with avalanches of all sizes. The question is whether the model has a scaling limit, a continuum object describing the avalanches, and what its critical exponents are. The exponents are estimated by simulation, but no scaling limit is proved and no exact exponents are known. The problem asks for the scaling limit and the exponents of the sandpile. The central equation is: Does the abelian sandpile model have a scaling limit, and what are its critical exponents? The sandpile models earthquakes, forest fires, the failure of power grids and the dynamics of granular materials: wherever a system drives itself to criticality and releases energy in avalanches, the same exponents are expected. A scaling limit would give a continuum theory of self-organised criticality, one of the most influential ideas in complex systems, and would provide precise predictions for the size distribution of avalanches. The problem is also a test of the methods of probability for non-equilibrium critical phenomena, where rigorous results are scarce.

Statement

The abelian sandpile model is a cellular automaton in which grains of sand are added to a lattice and any site with too many grains topples, sending one grain to each neighbour. The model exhibits self-organised criticality: it drives itself to a critical state with avalanches of all sizes. The question is whether the model has a scaling limit, a continuum object describing the avalanches, and what its critical exponents are. The exponents are estimated by simulation, but no scaling limit is proved and no exact exponents are known. The problem asks for the scaling limit and the exponents of the sandpile. The equation is: Does the abelian sandpile model have a scaling limit, and what are its critical exponents?

History

Bak, Tang and Wiesenfeld introduced self-organised criticality in 1987, and the abelian sandpile was introduced by Dhar in 1990 as the exactly analysable example. The model has a rich algebraic structure: the set of recurrent configurations forms a finite abelian group, and the stationary measure is uniform on that group. The connection to spanning trees, through the burning bijection of Majumdar and Dhar, gave exact results for some quantities. But the scaling limit of the avalanches and the critical exponents have resisted all methods, and the model remains without a continuum theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the abelian sandpile has a well-defined stationary measure, uniform on the recurrent configurations, and that the avalanche size distribution follows a power law by simulation, with exponents estimated numerically. The burning bijection connects the recurrent configurations to spanning trees, giving exact counts for some observables. The model is known to be critical in the sense that the avalanche distribution has no characteristic scale. What is not known is the existence of a scaling limit, the exact values of the avalanche exponents, or the continuum description of the avalanches: the problem is open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000022 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let S be a sandpile configuration on a box, and let an avalanche be the set of sites that topple after adding a grain. The avalanche size distribution is believed to follow a power law P(size > s) ~ s^{-tau} for some exponent tau. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an exponent tau and a scaling limit such that the rescaled avalanche clusters converge in distribution to a random continuum object, and the problem asks for the exact value of tau and the law of the limit. The statement is a claim about the scaling of a self-organised critical system.

Science Proof

The sandpile models earthquakes, forest fires, the failure of power grids and the dynamics of granular materials: wherever a system drives itself to criticality and releases energy in avalanches, the same exponents are expected. A scaling limit would give a continuum theory of self-organised criticality, one of the most influential ideas in complex systems, and would provide precise predictions for the size distribution of avalanches. The problem is also a test of the methods of probability for non-equilibrium critical phenomena, where rigorous results are scarce.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1022
N = 6
GRAINS = 40


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    grid = [[0] * N for _ in range(N)]
    topplings = 0
    for _ in range(GRAINS):
        x, y = rng.randrange(N), rng.randrange(N)
        grid[x][y] += 1
        stack = [(x, y)]
        while stack:
            cx, cy = stack.pop()
            while grid[cx][cy] >= 4:
                grid[cx][cy] -= 4
                topplings += 1
                for dx, dy in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
                    nx, ny = cx + dx, cy + dy
                    if 0 <= nx < N and 0 <= ny < N:
                        grid[nx][ny] += 1
                        if grid[nx][ny] >= 4:
                            stack.append((nx, ny))
    total = sum(sum(row) for row in grid)
    payload = "sand n=%d grains=%d topplings=%d total=%d" % (N, GRAINS, topplings, total)
    lines = ["Grid: %dx%d, grains added: %d" % (N, N, GRAINS),
             "Total topplings: %d" % topplings,
             "Grains remaining: %d" % total]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Random Geometric Graphs Chromatic Number JAH-USEQPR-000023 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

A random geometric graph is formed by scattering points uniformly in a square and joining pairs that are closer than a fixed radius. Its chromatic number, the smallest number of colours needed so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour, is known up to a constant factor, but the exact asymptotic value is unknown. The problem asks for the precise asymptotics: the constant in front of the leading term, the second-order term, and whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value as the number of points grows. The central equation is: What is the exact asymptotic chromatic number of a random geometric graph? Random geometric graphs model wireless networks, sensor networks and the spread of information in space: the chromatic number is the number of frequency channels needed to avoid interference, so its exact value determines the capacity of a wireless network. Precise asymptotics would give engineers exact predictions for channel allocation and would complete the theory of random geometric graphs, one of the main models of spatial networks. The problem also tests the methods of probabilistic combinatorics in a geometric setting, where the dependence between nearby points makes the analysis harder than in the purely random case.

Statement

A random geometric graph is formed by scattering points uniformly in a square and joining pairs that are closer than a fixed radius. Its chromatic number, the smallest number of colours needed so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour, is known up to a constant factor, but the exact asymptotic value is unknown. The problem asks for the precise asymptotics: the constant in front of the leading term, the second-order term, and whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value as the number of points grows. The equation is: What is the exact asymptotic chromatic number of a random geometric graph?

History

Random geometric graphs were introduced by Gilbert in 1961 to model wireless networks, and their theory was developed by Penrose and others in the 1990s and 2000s. The connectivity threshold was determined exactly, and the chromatic number was shown to be of order n r^2 / log n in the dense regime, where r is the connection radius. The clique number, which bounds the chromatic number from below, is known asymptotically, but the chromatic number itself has resisted exact determination: the gap between the clique number and the chromatic number is not understood, and the precise asymptotics remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the chromatic number of a random geometric graph is of order (n r^2)/(log n) in the dense regime, and that it is bounded below by the clique number, which is about (n r^2) / (2 log n) in the same regime. The connectivity threshold is exactly known, and the graph is known to have a sharp threshold for the appearance of a giant component. What is not known is the exact constant in the chromatic number asymptotics, the second-order term, or whether the chromatic number concentrates: the interval between the best lower and upper bounds has not been closed, and the problem is open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000023 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let points be scattered uniformly in the unit square, join pairs at distance at most r, and let chi be the chromatic number of the resulting graph. The problem asks for the exact asymptotic expansion of chi as the number of points n tends to infinity with r fixed in the dense regime. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function f(n) such that chi / f(n) tends to one in probability, and the problem asks for the exact function f, including its constant and second-order term. The statement is a claim about the precise asymptotics of a random geometric quantity.

Science Proof

Random geometric graphs model wireless networks, sensor networks and the spread of information in space: the chromatic number is the number of frequency channels needed to avoid interference, so its exact value determines the capacity of a wireless network. Precise asymptotics would give engineers exact predictions for channel allocation and would complete the theory of random geometric graphs, one of the main models of spatial networks. The problem also tests the methods of probabilistic combinatorics in a geometric setting, where the dependence between nearby points makes the analysis harder than in the purely random case.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

Random geometric graphs were introduced by Gilbert in 1961 to model wireless networks, and their theory was developed by Penrose and others in the 1990s and 2000s. The connectivity threshold was determined exactly, and the chromatic number was shown to be of order n r^2 / log n in the dense regime, where r is the connection radius. The clique number, which bounds the chromatic number from below, is known asymptotically, but the chromatic number itself has resisted exact determination: the gap between the clique number and the chromatic number is not understood, and the precise asymptotics remain open. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of probability, statistical physics and random structures, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

A random geometric graph is formed by scattering points uniformly in a square and joining pairs that are closer than a fixed radius. Its chromatic number, the smallest number of colours needed so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour, is known up to a constant factor, but the exact asymptotic value is unknown. The problem asks for the precise asymptotics: the constant in front of the leading term, the second-order term, and whether the chromatic number concentrates on a single value as the number of points grows. In symbols, the problem is: What is the exact asymptotic chromatic number of a random geometric graph? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from probability, statistical physics and random structures. It is known that the chromatic number of a random geometric graph is of order (n r^2)/(log n) in the dense regime, and that it is bounded below by the clique number, which is about (n r^2) / (2 log n) in the same regime. The connectivity threshold is exactly known, and the graph is known to have a sharp threshold for the appearance of a giant component. What is not known is the exact constant in the chromatic number asymptotics, the second-order term, or whether the chromatic number concentrates: the interval between the best lower and upper bounds has not been closed, and the problem is open. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Verification path

A reader can check the claims in this record by consulting the sources cited, each of which establishes the part of the story it is cited for, and by running the demo and the program, which reproduce the small verified computations exactly. Independent verification of the open status would mean finding a proof or a counterexample in the peer-reviewed literature: no such item exists for this problem, and the record would have to be revised if one appeared. The demo is deterministic, so its output can be reproduced byte for byte on any machine with a standard Python installation.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the problem be stated in the language of probability, statistical physics and random structures. The statement is: What is the exact asymptotic chromatic number of a random geometric graph? The gloss is a formal rendering of the informal statement: it quantifies over the objects the problem concerns, it states the required property exactly, and it leaves no room for a reading under which a partial result would count as a solution. A proof would have to establish the glossed statement in full; a refutation would have to exhibit a counterexample to it. The formal gloss is the contract that any claimed answer must satisfy.

What is not known is the exact constant in the chromatic number asymptotics, the second-order term, or whether the chromatic number concentrates: the interval between the best lower and upper bounds has not been closed, and the problem is open. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. Working demo program

import hashlib
import random

SEED = 1023
N = 10
R = 0.35


def run():
    rng = random.Random(SEED)
    pts = [(rng.random(), rng.random()) for _ in range(N)]
    adj = {i: set() for i in range(N)}
    edges = 0
    for i in range(N):
        for j in range(i + 1, N):
            d = ((pts[i][0] - pts[j][0]) ** 2 + (pts[i][1] - pts[j][1]) ** 2) ** 0.5
            if d <= R:
                adj[i].add(j)
                adj[j].add(i)
                edges += 1
    order = sorted(range(N), key=lambda v: -len(adj[v]))
    best = [N]

    def search(k, col, used_max):
        if used_max >= best[0]:
            return
        if k == N:
            best[0] = used_max
            return
        v = order[k]
        taken = {col[u] for u in adj[v] if u in col}
        for c in range(used_max + 1):
            if c not in taken:
                col[v] = c
                search(k + 1, col, max(used_max, c + 1))
                del col[v]

    search(0, {}, 0)
    payload = "rgg n=%d r=%s edges=%d chi=%d" % (N, R, edges, best[0])
    lines = ["Points: %d, connection radius: %s" % (N, R),
             "Edges: %d" % edges,
             "Exact chromatic number: %d" % best[0]]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Decorated Random Planar Maps Scaling Limits JAH-USEQPR-000024 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

A random planar map is a random graph drawn on the sphere, and a decorated map carries extra structure, such as Ising spins or a percolation configuration, on its faces or vertices. The undecorated maps are understood: they converge to the Brownian map, by the work of Le Gall and Miermont. The decorated maps are believed to converge to Liouville quantum gravity surfaces coupled to matter, with exponents depending on the decoration, but no scaling limit is proved for the main decorated models. The problem asks for the scaling limits of random planar maps with matter, and for the exact exponents that govern them. The central equation is: What are the scaling limits of random planar maps coupled to matter, such as the Ising or percolation decoration? Random planar maps with matter model two-dimensional quantum gravity coupled to matter fields: they are the discrete version of the theory that physicists use to describe the geometry of spacetime at the smallest scales. Their scaling limits would give a rigorous theory of quantum gravity in two dimensions, with precise predictions for the geometry of random surfaces. The problem also connects probability, combinatorics and physics, and its solution would complete the programme of understanding random geometry that began with Tutte's enumeration of maps in the 1960s.

Statement

A random planar map is a random graph drawn on the sphere, and a decorated map carries extra structure, such as Ising spins or a percolation configuration, on its faces or vertices. The undecorated maps are understood: they converge to the Brownian map, by the work of Le Gall and Miermont. The decorated maps are believed to converge to Liouville quantum gravity surfaces coupled to matter, with exponents depending on the decoration, but no scaling limit is proved for the main decorated models. The problem asks for the scaling limits of random planar maps with matter, and for the exact exponents that govern them. The equation is: What are the scaling limits of random planar maps coupled to matter, such as the Ising or percolation decoration?

History

Random planar maps were introduced by Tutte in the 1960s, and their scaling limit, the Brownian map, was constructed by Le Gall and Miermont in the 2010s. The physics of maps coupled to matter was developed by Kazakov, Kostov and others in the 1980s using matrix models, which predicted the KPZ exponents for the decorated models. The mathematical theory of Liouville quantum gravity, developed by Duplantier, Sheffield, Miller and others in the 2010s, gave a candidate continuum object. But the convergence of the discrete decorated maps to the Liouville quantum gravity surfaces remains unproven for the main models, such as maps with Ising spins.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that undecorated random planar maps converge to the Brownian map, and that the Brownian map has a well-understood geometry, including the confluence of geodesics. The KPZ formula relating the exponents of the decorated models to those of the undecorated ones is proved in the continuum setting by Duplantier and Sheffield. What is not known is the convergence of the discrete decorated maps to their continuum limits: the Ising-decorated maps, the percolation-decorated maps and the maps with a spanning tree are all conjectured to converge to specific Liouville quantum gravity surfaces, but no proof exists for the main cases.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPRS-000024 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let M_n be a random planar map with n faces, decorated with Ising spins at the critical temperature, and rescale the graph distance by n^{-1/4}. The conjecture is that M_n converges in distribution to a Liouville quantum gravity surface with a specific parameter, in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a random metric space X such that the rescaled decorated maps converge in distribution to X, and the problem asks for the law of X and the exact exponents of the decoration. The statement is a convergence claim for random decorated surfaces.

Science Proof

Random planar maps with matter model two-dimensional quantum gravity coupled to matter fields: they are the discrete version of the theory that physicists use to describe the geometry of spacetime at the smallest scales. Their scaling limits would give a rigorous theory of quantum gravity in two dimensions, with precise predictions for the geometry of random surfaces. The problem also connects probability, combinatorics and physics, and its solution would complete the programme of understanding random geometry that began with Tutte's enumeration of maps in the 1960s.

Working demo program

import hashlib

N = 6


def run():
    verts = list(range(N))
    edges = []
    for i in range(N):
        edges.append((i, (i + 1) % N))
        edges.append((i, (i + 2) % N))
    e = len(set(tuple(sorted(p)) for p in edges))
    faces = 2 - N + e
    payload = "map verts=%d edges=%d faces=%d" % (N, e, faces)
    lines = ["Vertices: %d" % N,
             "Edges (circulant graph): %d" % e,
             "Faces by Euler formula V - E + F = 2: %d" % faces]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
